Union of Finitely Generated Congruences on Ground Term Algebra
Sándor Vágvölgyi

Abstract
We show that for any ground term equation systems E E and F F , (1) the union of the generated congruences by E E and F F is a congruence on the ground term algebra if and only if there exists a ground term equation system H H such that the congruence generated by H H is equal to the union of the congruences generated by E E and F F if and only if the congruence generated by the union of E E and F F is equal to the union of the congruences generated by E E and F F , and (2) it is decidable in square time whether the congruence generated by the union of E E and F F is equal to the union of the congruences generated by E E and F F , where the size of the input is the number of occurrences of symbols in E E plus the number of occurrences of symbols in F F .



Journal:
Journal of Automata, Languages and Combinatorics

1
Introduction

Kozen
[
1
]
, Nelson and Oppen
[
2
]
, and Tarjan
[
3
]
studied the word problem for ground term equation systems (GTESs)
and applied the graph-based method of congruence closure. In order to decide for a given GTES
E
and ground terms
s
and
t
, whether
s\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}t
holds,
they constructed the subterm graph of
E
and
s
and
t
and computed the congruential consequences of
E
on the vertices of the graph.
Kozen
[
1
]

showed that the word problem for every GTES is decidable in polynomial
time. Nelson and Oppen
[
2
]

and Tarjan
[
3
]
showed that the
word problem for every GTES is decidable in
O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
time applying the congruence closure procedure on the subterm graph of the GTES
E
and ground terms
s
and
t
.
Continuing this research line,
Snyder
[
4
]
gave an efficient algorithm
which, given a GTES
E
over a signature
\Sigma
, produces an equivalent, reduced ground term rewriting system
R
over
\Sigma
in
O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
time. Then
Fülöp and Vágvölgyi
[
5
]
gave a simple and efficient algorithm
which, given a GTES
E
over a signature
\Sigma
, produces a reduced ground term rewriting system (GTRS for short)
R
over a signature containing
\Sigma
such that two terms are congruent modulo
E
if and only if their
R
-normal forms are equal.
The algorithm calls a congruence closure procedure

[
3
]
on the subterm graph of the GTES
E
and runs in
O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
time, where
n
is the size of
E
.
We say that a tree language is congruential if it is the union of finitely many congruence classes of a finitely generated congruence. Brainerd
[
6
]
, Kozen
[
1
]

showed that congruential tree languages are the same as recognizable tree languages. Applying the above algorithm of Fülöp and Vágvölgyi
[
5
]
,
for a given GTES
E
and ground terms
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}
of size
n
, a
deterministic bottom-up tree automaton
can be constructed in
O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
time which recognizes the
congruential tree language
p_{1}/_{\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}}\cup\cdots\cup p_{k}/_{\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}}

[
7
]
.

Vágvölgyi
[
8
]

showed that it is decidable for any given
GTESs
E
and
F
whether
there is a GTES
H
such that

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{H}=\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cap\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
. If the answer is
“yes”
, then we can construct such a GTES
H
.
We show the dual to the above result, first we
show that
for any GTESs
E
and
F
,
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}

is a congruence on the ground term algebra
if and only if
there exists a GTES
H
such that
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{H}=\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}

if and only if
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}=\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
.
Second, we show that it is decidable in
O(n^{2})

time for any given
GTESs
E
and
F
whether
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}=\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
, where

n
is the number of occurrences of symbols in
E
plus the number of occurrences of symbols in
F
.
For each signature
\Sigma
without constants, the set of ground terms over
\Sigma
is the empty set, each GTES over
\Sigma
is the empty set, and our results above are obvious. Thus, throughout the paper we consider signatures with constants.

We now recall the fast ground completion algorithm of Fülöp and Vágvölgyi
[
5
]
.
Let

E
and
F
be GTESs over
\Sigma
, and let
W
denote the set of subterms appearing in the GTESs
E
and
F
.
Fülöp and Vágvölgyi
[
5
]

presented a
fast ground completion algorithm which, given GTESs
E
and
F
over a signature
\Sigma
,
produces in
O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
time a finite set
C\langle E,F\rangle
of new constants and
a reduced GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle

over
\Sigma
extended with the new constants
such that
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}=\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}\cap T_{\Sigma}\times T_{\Sigma}
.
Here
n
stands for the number of occurrences of symbols in
E
plus the number of occurrences of symbols in
F
.
Furthermore, the set
C\langle E,F\rangle
of new constants is the set of
the equivalence classes of
the restriction
\Theta\langle E,F\rangle

of
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}
to the set
W
. For this purpose we proceed as follows:
In the first step in
O(n)

time, we create a subterm directed, labeled, and acyclic multigraph (dag)
G
for
E
and
F

[
4
]
. We consider the vertices in the subterm dag
G
as terms in
W
.
The relation
\tau\langle E,F\rangle
on the vertices of
G
corresponds to
E
. We construct the relation
\tau\langle E,F\rangle
.
Then
we run on
G
a congruence closure algorithm for the relation
\tau\langle E,F\rangle
in
O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
time
[
9
]
. In this way, we obtain on

G
the congruence closure
\rho\langle E,F\rangle
of the relation
\tau\langle E,F\rangle
. Considering the vertices in the subterm dag
G
as terms,

\rho\langle E,F\rangle
yields
the restriction
\Theta\langle E,F\rangle
of
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}
to
W
. Then using
\rho\langle E,F\rangle
,

\Theta\langle E,F\rangle
, and
C\langle E,F\rangle
, we
produce the GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
in
O(n)
time.
GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
is the set of all
rewrite rules

\sigma({b}_{1},\ldots,{b}_{m})\dot{\rightarrow}{b}
, where

\sigma\in\Sigma_{m}
,
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
{b}_{1},\ldots,{b}_{m},{b}\in C\langle E,F\rangle
, and
\{\,\sigma(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})\mid t_{1}\in{b}_{1},\ldots,t_{m}\in{b}_{m}\,\}\subseteq{b}
.
Fülöp and Vágvölgyi
[
5
]
showed that GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
is reduced, and hence is convergent.
We construct GTRSs
R\langle F,E\rangle
and
R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
in a similar way.

The intuitive idea of the paper is as follows. Let

E
and
F
be GTESs over
\Sigma
. For every subterm
t
appearing in
E\cup F
,
GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
reduces all elements of the congruence class
t/_{\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{\langle E,F\rangle}}

to the constant (equivalence class)
t/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
,
and
GTRS
R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
reduces every element of the congruence class
t/_{\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle}}

to the constant (equivalence class)
t/_{\Theta\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle}
.
For every subterm
t
appearing in
E\cup F
, the congruence class
t/_{{\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle}}}
is equal to the congruence class
t/_{{\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{\langle E,F\rangle}}}

if and only if
t/_{\Theta\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle}=t/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
and
for every
p\in T_{\Sigma}
and reduction sequence
{\cal RS}1
from
p
to
t/_{\Theta\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle}
by

R\langle{E\cup F},\emptyset\rangle
,
there exists a reduction sequence
{\cal RS}2
from
p
to
t/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
by

R\langle E,F\rangle
,
where
each rewrite rule

\sigma({b}_{1},\ldots,{b}_{m})\dot{\rightarrow}{b}\in R\langle{E\cup F},\emptyset\rangle
applied at any node of
p
along the reduction sequence
{\cal RS}1
is matched with
some rewrite rule

\sigma({c}_{1},\ldots,{c}_{m})\dot{\rightarrow}{c}
in
R\langle E,F\rangle

applied by GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
at the same node along the reduction sequence
{\cal RS}2
. By matching we mean that

{c}_{i}\subseteq{b}_{i}

for all
i\in\{\,1,\ldots,m\,\}
,
and

{c}\subseteq{b}
.
For every
{b}\in C\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
,
we say that GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
keeps up with the GTRS

R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
writing the constant
{b}

if the following holds.
If GTRS

R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
applies a rewrite rule of the form

\sigma({b}_{1},\ldots,{b}_{m})\dot{\rightarrow}{b}
along a reductions sequence from
p
to
t/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
, then

R\langle E,F\rangle
also applies a rewrite rule
\sigma({c}_{1},\ldots,{c}_{m})\dot{\rightarrow}{c}
for some

{c}_{1},\ldots,{c}_{m},c\in C\langle E,F\rangle
at the same node, that is,
R\langle E,F\rangle
also move forward along its reduction sequence.
For every
{b}\in C\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
,
we define that GTRS
R\langle F,E\rangle
keeps up with the GTRS

R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
writing the constant
{b}

in a similar way.

For each constant

{d}\in C\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
, we keep track of all constants
{c}\in C\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
which appear as a right-hand side of a rewrite rule applied along
the reduction sequence from some ground term
t\in T_{\Sigma}
to the constant

{d}
by
R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
.
To this end, we construct a directed pseudograph without parallel arcs (dpwpa),
AUX[E;F]
with vertices
C\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
. At each vertex
{b}\in C\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
, we indicate whether it is an element of
C\langle E,F\rangle
whether it is an
element of

C\langle F,E\rangle
, whether GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
keeps up with GTRS
R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
writing the constant
{b}
, and whether GTRS
R\langle F,E\rangle
keeps up with GTRS
R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
writing the constant
{b}
. Every directed arc
({b},{c})
is associated with some rewrite rule

\sigma({b}_{1},\ldots,{b}_{m})\dot{\rightarrow}{b}
in
R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
, where
{c}={b}_{i}
for some
i\in\{\,1,\ldots,m\,\}
. That is,
{b}
is the right-hand side of the
rewrite rule, and
{c}
appears on the left-hand side of the rewrite rule.
We say that GTRS

R\langle E,F\rangle

simulates the GTRS
R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
on a constant
{d}\in C\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
if
{d}\in C\langle E,F\rangle
and
for all constants
{c}
appearing in a reduction sequence from a ground term
p\in T_{\Sigma}
to
{d}
by GTRS
R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
, GTRS

R\langle E,F\rangle
keeps up with GTRS
R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
writing
c
.
That is, for all constants
{c}
with a path of positive length or a cycle from

{c}
to
{d}
in
AUX[E;F]
,

R\langle E,F\rangle
keeps up with GTRS
R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
writing
c
.
We define that GTRS

R\langle F,E\rangle

simulates the GTRS
R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
on a constant
{d}\in C\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
in a similar way.
During the run of the decision algorithm, we carry out a partial depth first search on the auxiliary dpwpa
AUX[E;F]
to decide whether
R\langle E,F\rangle

simulates the GTRS
R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
on a constant
{d}\in C\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
, and
to decide whether
R\langle F,E\rangle

simulates the GTRS
R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
on a constant
{d}\in C\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
.
We say that GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
and GTRS
R\langle F,E\rangle

together simulate the GTRS
R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle

if
for each constant
{d}\in C\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
, GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
or GTRS
R\langle F,E\rangle

simulate the GTRS
R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
on
{d}
. We show that
for every subterm
t
appearing in
E\cup F
,

\left(t/_{\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}}=t/_{{\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}}}\mbox{ or }t/_{\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}}=t/_{{\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}}}\right)

if and only if
GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
and GTRS
R\langle F,E\rangle

together simulate the GTRS
R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
.
We consider the following four main cases.

Main Case 1:
\Sigma
is a unary signature. Then
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}=\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
if and only if GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
and GTRS
R\langle F,E\rangle

together simulate the GTRS
R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
.

Main Case 2: Both GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
and GTRS
R\langle F,E\rangle
are total.
Then
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}=\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}

if and only if each
\Theta\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
equivalence class is equal to a

\Theta\langle E,F\rangle
equivalence class or a
\Theta\langle F,E\rangle
equivalence class.

Main Case 3:
\Sigma_{k}\neq\emptyset
for some
k\geq 2
and
R\langle E,F\rangle

or
R\langle F,E\rangle
is total. Then
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}=\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
if and only if GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
and GTRS
R\langle F,E\rangle

together simulate the GTRS
R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
.

Main Case 4:
\Sigma
has a symbol of arity at least
2
,
and GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
and GTRS
R\langle F,E\rangle
are not total.
Then
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}=\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}

if and only if
E\subseteq\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
or

F\subseteq\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}
.

In Main Case 1, the overall complexity of our computation is
O(n^{2})
.
In each of Main Cases 2, 3, and 4, the overall complexity of our computation is
O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
.
Main Case 2 overlaps with Main Case 1 and Main Case 3, and is a subcase of the union of
Main Case 1 and Main Case 3.

We now describe an alternative approach for our decision problem.
We can think of the GTRSs
R\langle E,F\rangle
,
R\langle F,E\rangle
, and
R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle

as the sets of rewrite rules of bottom-up tree automata. Thence we can also apply the decision algorithm of Champavère et al
[
10
]
. Their
algorithm decides inclusion between two tree languages recognized by given bottom-up
tree automata. Applying their algorithm to our decision problem in each of Main Cases 1, 2, and 3 yields an alternative decision algorithm, which
is more sophisticated than ours, and in general takes more time than ours, it runs in
O(n^{3})
time.

For any given
GTESs
E
and
F
, we can construct the GTES
E\cup F
in
O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
time, where

n
is as above.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows:
In Section
2
, we recall some basic definitions and fix the notations. In Section

3
, we start our running examples.
We consider in each main case an example of GTESs
E
and
F
such that

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}=\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
and an example
for GTESs
E
and
F
such that

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}\subset\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}
.
In Section
4
, we recall the fast ground completion algorithm of
Fülöp and Vágvölgyi
[
5
]
.
In Section
5
, we continue our running examples.
In Section
6
, we
define the auxiliary dpwpa
AUX[E;F]=(C\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle,A[E;F])

for the GTESs
E
and
F
.
Then we compute
AUX[E;F]
for our running examples.
In Section
7
, we further continue our running examples. In Section
8
,
we construct the auxiliary dpwpa for the GTESs
E
and
F
.
In Section
9
, we show some preparatory results. In Section
10
, we define the notion
that GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
and GTRS
R\langle F,E\rangle

together simulate the GTRS
R\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
. In Section
11
, we
define the four main cases of our decision algorithm. We show that we can decide which Main Cases hold in
O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
time.
In Sections
12
,
13
,
14
, and
15
, we study Main Cases 1, 2, 3, and 4, respectively
one by one.
For each main case, we present our decidability and complexity results, and we continue our relevant running examples.
In Section
16
, we sum up our decidability and complexity results.
In Section
17
we present our concluding remarks.

2
Preliminaries

In this section, we present a brief review of the notions, notation and
preliminary results used in the paper.

Algorithm concepts

In algorithmic analysis we use the RAM (random access machine) model
[
11
, Section 2.2]
,

[
12
, Section 2.1]
.
For this model of computation,
each simple operation
(+,\,*,\,\textendash,\,=,\,if,\,call)
takes exactly one time step.
An array is a collection of items of same data type stored at contiguous memory locations.
For the concept of a red-black tree, see
[
13
, Section 13]
.

Proposition 2.1
.

[
13
, Section 13]

The search operation takes
O(\mathrm{log}\,n)
time on a red-black tree, where
n
is the size of the tree.

Proposition 2.2
.

[
13
, Section 13]

The time-complexity of inserting
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
elements into an empty red-black tree, not allowing duplicate values in the resulting red-black tree, is
O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
.

Proof.

The time-complexity of inserting a single element into a red-black tree is
O(\mathrm{log}\,n)
,
where
n
is the size of the tree
[
13
]
. Consequently it takes
O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
time to insert
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
elements into an empty red-black tree.
We modify the insertion routine of the red-black tree to not allow duplicate values. The insertion function takes the same amount of time i.e.
O(\mathrm{log}\,n)
,
as before, and does not insert a value if it is already in the tree.
∎

Basic notions

The set of nonnegative integers is denoted by
{\mathbb{N}}
, and
{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
stands
for the free monoid generated by
{\mathbb{N}}
with empty word

\varepsilon
as identity element. The length of a word
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
is defined as the number of integers it contains and is
denoted by

\mathrm{length}(\alpha)
.
A word
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
is a prefix of a word
\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
if there is a word
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}

such that
\alpha\gamma=\beta
. Moreover, if
\gamma\neq\varepsilon
, then
\alpha
is a proper prefix of
\beta
.

A binary sequence is a string of ones and zeros,
BIN
stands for the set of binary sequences.
For an integer
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we write
BIN_{\ell}

to denote the set of binary sequences
of length
\ell
. We define the binary sequence
0^{k}
,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
by recursion on
k
. Let
0^{0}=\varepsilon
. For each
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let

0^{k+1}=00^{k}
.
Let
0\leq k\leq\ell
and
\alpha\in BIN_{k}
. We pad the binary sequence
\alpha
with leading zeros to length
\ell
by
producing the binary sequence
0^{\ell-k}\alpha
.

We now recall the definition of the lexicographic order on
BIN

[
14
, Definition 6.4.1.]
.
Given two distinct binary strings, to determine whether one precedes the other in the lexicographic order
we compare the numbers in the words. There are two possibilities:

1.

The words have different lengths, and each number in the shorter word
is identical to the corresponding number in the longer word.

2.

The words have the same or different lengths, and at some
position, the numbers in the words differ.

If Condition 1 holds, then the shorter word precedes the longer. If Condition 2 holds, we locate the leftmost position
\ell
at which the
numbers differ. The order of the words is determined by the order of the numbers at position
\ell
.

Definition 2.3

(
[
14
, Definition 6.4.1.]
)
.

Let
\alpha=i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{m}
and
\beta=j_{1}j_{2}\ldots j_{n}
be binary strings. We say that
\alpha
is lexicographically less than
\beta
and write

\alpha<_{lex}\beta
if

1.

m<n
and
i_{k}=j_{k}
for every
k=1,2,\ldots,m

or

2.

i_{k}\neq j_{k}
for some
k
and for the smallest such
k
, we have
i_{k}<j_{k}
.

Sets and Relations
[
15
]

For any sets
A
and
B
,

A\subseteq B
means that
A
is a subset of
B
. Proper inclusion is denoted by
A\subset B
. The union of two sets
A
and
B
is denoted by
A\cup B
.
The cardinality of a set
A
is denoted
|A|
.
A relation on a set
A
is a subset

{\rightarrow}
of
A\times A

[
15
]
.
We write
{b}{\rightarrow}{c}
for
({b},{c})\in\,{\rightarrow}
.
We denote by
{\rightarrow}^{*}
the reflexive
transitive closure of
{\rightarrow}
,
by
\leftrightarrow^{+}
the transitive symmetric closure of
{\rightarrow}
,
and by
\leftrightarrow^{*}
the reflexive
transitive symmetric closure of
{\rightarrow}
.
Note that
\leftrightarrow^{*}
is an
equivalence relation on
A
.

Let
{\rightarrow}
be a relation on a set
A
.
An element
{b}\in A
is in normal form (irreducible for
{\rightarrow}
) if there
exists no
{c}\in A
such that

{b}{\rightarrow}{c}
.
We say that

{c}\in A
is a normal form of
{b}\in A
if
{b}{\rightarrow}^{*}{c}
and
{c}
is in normal form.

A relation
{\rightarrow}
on a set
A
is

1.

normalizing if every element has a normal form,

2.

terminating if there exists no infinite sequence of
elements
{b}_{1},{b}_{2},{b}_{3},\ldots
in
A

such that
{b}_{1}{\rightarrow}{b}_{2}{\rightarrow}{b}_{3}{\rightarrow}\ldots
,

3.

confluent if for any elements
{b}_{1},{b}_{2},{b}_{3}
in
A
,
whenever
{b}_{1}{\rightarrow}^{*}{b}_{2}
and
{b}_{1}{\rightarrow}^{*}{b}_{3}
, there exists an
element
{b}_{4}
in
A
such that
{b}_{2}{\rightarrow}^{*}{b}_{4}
and
{b}_{3}{\rightarrow}^{*}{b}_{4}
,

4.

convergent if it is terminating and confluent.

Any terminating relation is normalizing. Hence we have the following proposition.

Proposition 2.4

(
[
15
, Lemma 2.1.8]
)
.

For any convergent relation
{\rightarrow}
on a set
A
, every

{b}\in A
has a unique normal form, called the normal form of
{b}
.

If
{b}\in A
has a unique normalform, then we denote the latter
by
{b}{\downarrow}
.

Let
\rho
be an
equivalence relation on
A
. Then for
every
{b}\in A
, we denote by
{b}/_{\rho}

the
\rho
equivalence class containing
{b}
,
i.e.
{b}/_{\rho}=\{\,{c}\mid{b}\rho{c}\,\}
.
For each
B\subseteq A
, let
B/_{\rho}=\{\,b/_{\rho}\mid b\in B\,\}
.

The empty product
\Pi_{i=1}^{0}n_{i}=1
over
{\mathbb{N}}

is the result of multiplying no factors.
The ceiling function maps a real number
x
to the least integer greater than or equal to
x
, denoted
\lceil x\rceil
.

Ground terms
[
15
]

A signature
\Sigma
is a finite set of symbols in
which every element
\sigma
is associated with a non-negative integer, the arity of
\sigma
denoted by
\mathrm{arity}(\sigma)
.
For each integer
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\Sigma_{k}

denotes the elements of
\Sigma
which have arity
k
. We lose no generality assuming that
\Sigma_{0}\not=\emptyset
, see the Introduction.
If
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma_{1}
, then
\Sigma
is a unary signature.

mar_{\Sigma}
denotes the maximal arity of the symbols in
\Sigma
.

We denote by
T_{\Sigma}
the set of ground terms over
\Sigma
. It is the smallest set

U
for which

(i)

\Sigma_{0}\subseteq U
and

(ii)

\sigma(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})\in U
whenever
\sigma\in\Sigma_{m}
with
m\geq 1
and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}\in U
.

Ground terms are also called trees. A set of ground terms is also called a tree language.
For a ground term
t\in T_{\Sigma}
, the set
sub(t)
of subterms
of
t
is defined by recursion as follows:

(i)

if
t\in\Sigma_{0}
, then

sub(t)=\{\,t\,\}
,

(ii)

if
t=\sigma(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})

for some
m\geq 1
,
\sigma\in\Sigma_{m}
, and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}\in T_{\Sigma}
,
then we have
sub(t)=\bigcup\{\,sub(t_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq m\,\}\cup\{\,t\,\}
.

For a finite set
Z\subseteq T_{\Sigma}
of ground terms,

sub(Z)=\bigcup\{\,sub(t)\mid t\in Z\,\}
.

For a ground term
t\in T_{\Sigma}
, the height of
t
is denoted by

height(t)
and is defined by recursion:

(i)

if
t\in\Sigma_{0}
, then

height(t)=0
, and

(ii)

if
t=\sigma(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
with
m\geq 1
and
\sigma\in\Sigma_{m}
, then

height(t)=max\{\,height(t_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq m\,\}+1
.

For a ground term
t\in T_{\Sigma}
, the set of positions
\mathrm{POS}(t)\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}^{*}

of
t
is defined by tree induction.

1.

If
t\in\Sigma_{0},
then
\mathrm{POS}(t)=\{\,\varepsilon\,\}
.

2.

If
t=\sigma(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
with
\sigma\in\Sigma_{m}
,
m\geq 1
, then

\mathrm{POS}(t)=\{\,\varepsilon\,\}\cup\{\,i\alpha\mid 1\leq i\leq m,\alpha\in\mathrm{POS}(t_{i})\,\}
.

The size of a ground term
t\in T_{\Sigma}
is
\mathrm{size}(t)=|POS(t)|
.
For a finite set
Z\subseteq T_{\Sigma}
of ground terms,

\mathrm{size}(Z)
is the number of occurrences of symbols of
\Sigma
in
Z
, i.e.,

\mathrm{size}(Z)=\sum_{t\in Z}\mathrm{size}(t)
.
For any ground term
t\in T_{\Sigma}
and position
\alpha\in\mathrm{POS}(t)
, we introduce the subterm

t|_{\alpha}\in T_{\Sigma}
of
t
at
\alpha

as follows:

1.

for
t\in\Sigma_{0}
,
t|_{\varepsilon}=t
;

2.

for
t=\sigma(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
with
m\geq 1
and
\sigma\in\Sigma_{m}
if

\alpha=\varepsilon
then
t|_{\alpha}=t
,
otherwise if

\alpha=i\beta
with
1\leq i\leq m
, then
t|_{\alpha}=t_{i}|_{\beta}
.

A context
\delta
over
\Sigma
can be viewed as an incomplete term, which may contain several empty places or holes.
Formally, a context over
\Sigma
is a ground term containing zero, one or more occurrences of a special constant symbol

\diamondsuit\not\in\Sigma

denoting holes, i.e., a ground term over the extended signature
\Sigma\cup\{\,\diamondsuit\,\}
. If
\delta
contains exactly
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
holes, then we say that
\delta
is an
n
-context.
The set of all
n
-contexts over
\Sigma

is denoted by
CON_{\Sigma,n}
.
For example, let
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma_{2}
,
\Sigma_{0}=\{\,\#,\$\,\}
, and
\Sigma_{2}=\{\,f,g\,\}
.
Then

g(f(\diamondsuit,\#),g(\diamondsuit,\diamondsuit))\in CON_{\Sigma,3}
.
Let
\delta
be an
n
-context over
\Sigma
, and let

t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\in T_{\Sigma}
be ground terms. Then
\delta[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
denotes the result of replacing the holes of
\delta
from left to right by
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
.
For example,

g(f(\diamondsuit,\#),f(\diamondsuit,\diamondsuit))[g(\$,\$),\#,\$]=g(f(g(\$,\$),\#),f(\#,\$))
.

Let
\delta
be a
1
-context over
\Sigma
, and let
\alpha\in POS(\delta)

be such that

t|_{\alpha}=\diamondsuit
. Then we call
\alpha
the address of
\diamondsuit
in
\delta
, and
addr(\delta)
stands for
\alpha
.
Let
\delta
be a
1
-context over
\Sigma
such that
\delta\neq\diamondsuit
, and let
{d}\in\Sigma_{0}
be a constant.
Then we say that the ground term
\delta[{d}]
is a proper extension of the constant
{d}\in\Sigma_{0}
.

Ground term rewrite systems and equation systems
[
4
]

Let
\Sigma
be a signature, and
R
be
a finite subset of
T_{\Sigma}\times T_{\Sigma}
.
The size of
R
is denoted by
\mathrm{size}(R)
, and is the number of
occurrences of symbols of
\Sigma
in
R
, ie.,
\mathrm{size}(R)=\sum_{(l,r)\in R}\mathrm{size}(l)+\mathrm{size}(r)
.
Let

\Sigma\langle R\rangle
denote the signature of those symbols which occur in
R
.
For any ground terms
s,t\in T_{\Sigma}
, we say that

s
rewrites to
t
, and denote this by
s\mathop{\rightarrow}_{R}t
if
there exist an ordered pair
(l,r)
in
R
, a context
\delta\in CON_{\Sigma,1}
such that

s=\delta[l]
and
t=\delta[r]
.
We fully specify such a rewrite step by

s\rightarrow_{[\alpha,(l,r)]}t
, where
\alpha=addr(\delta)
.
Furthermore,

s\leftrightarrow_{[\alpha,(l,r)]}t
stands for
(
s\rightarrow_{[\alpha,(l,r)]}t
or
t\rightarrow_{[\alpha,(l,r)]}s
).
When we focus on the relations
\mathop{\rightarrow}_{R}
and
\mathop{\rightarrow}_{R}^{*}
, we call
R
a ground term rewrite system (GTRS for short), and
we call an ordered pair
(l,r)
a rewrite rule, and denote it as
l\dot{\rightarrow}r
.
Moreover, we say that
l
is the
left-hand side and
r
is the right-hand side of the rewrite rule

l\dot{\rightarrow}r
.
When we focus on the symmetric relations
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}_{R}
and
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{R}
,
we call
R
a ground term equation system (GTES for short), and
we denote
(l,r)
as
l\doteq r
, and call it an equation. Moreover, we denote a GTES as
E
rather than
R

[
4
]
.
Let
E
and
F
be GTESs over a signature
\Sigma
. Then we define the GTES
E\cup F
over
\Sigma
as the union of the sets
E
and
F
.

Statement 2.5
.

Let
E
and
F
be GTESs over a signature
\Sigma
.
We can construct the GTES
E\cup F
over
\Sigma
in
O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
time.

Proof.

In

O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
time we mergesort the GTES
E
and the GTES
F

[
16
, Section 2.2]
. Then in
O(n)

time, we concurrently scan through both of the sorted relations to produce the result. When the concurrent scan of both GTESs reveals the same equation in both relations, only one of the equations is retained.
∎

Definition 2.6
.

Let
E
and
F
be GTESs over a signature
\Sigma
.
Then let
\Sigma\langle E,F\rangle=\Sigma\langle E\rangle\cup\Sigma\langle F\rangle
. That is,
\Sigma\langle E,F\rangle
denotes the signature of those symbols which occur in
E
or
F
.
The Boolean value
FLAG\langle E,F\rangle
indicates whether

\Sigma=\Sigma\langle E,F\rangle
.

Statement 2.7
.

Let
E
and
F
be GTESs over a signature
\Sigma
, and
n=\mathrm{size}(E)+\mathrm{size}(F)
.
We can count the elements of
\Sigma\langle E,F\rangle
and compute the Boolean value
FLAG\langle E,F\rangle
in
O(n)
time.

Let
E
be a GTES over
\Sigma
. Then

ST\langle E\rangle=\{\,t\in T_{\Sigma}\mid t\in sub(u)\mbox{ or }t\in sub(v)\mbox{ for some }u\doteq v\mbox{ in }E\,\}

is the set of subterms occurring
E
.
The
below claim is immediate.

Statement 2.8
.

1. Let
Z\subseteq T_{\Sigma}
be a finite set of ground terms over a signature
\Sigma
. Then
|sub(Z)|\leq\mathrm{size}(Z)
.

2. Let
E
be a GTES over a signature
\Sigma
. Then

|ST\langle E\rangle|\leq\mathrm{size}(E)
.

Let
\Sigma
be a signature.
A
\Sigma
-algebra
{\cal A}
consists of a carrier set
A
, and a mapping that associates with each symbol
\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}
a mapping

\sigma^{\cal A}:A^{k}\rightarrow A
for every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}

[
15
, Definition 3.2.1]
.
The
\Sigma
-ground-term algebra
{\cal T}(\Sigma)
has
T_{\Sigma}
as carrier set, and interprets the symbols
\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}
for every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
as follows:

\sigma^{{\cal T}(\Sigma)}:T_{\Sigma}^{k}\rightarrow T_{\Sigma}:(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\mapsto\sigma(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})
.

Let
E
be a GTES over
\Sigma
.
The congruence relation generated by
E
on the
\Sigma
-ground term algebra
{\cal T}(\Sigma)
is
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}
. It is well known that
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}
is the least congruence on
{\cal T}(\Sigma)

containing
E
.
Let
R
be a GTRS over
\Sigma
.
We say that GTRS
R
is equivalent to the GTES
E
if
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{R}=\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}
.
We say that GTRS
R
reaches a ground term
s\in T_{\Sigma}
from a ground term
t\in T_{\Sigma}
if
t\mathop{\rightarrow}_{R}^{*}s
holds.
We say that GTRS
R
is terminating, (confluent, etc.) if
\mathop{\rightarrow}_{R}
is
terminating (confluent, etc.). A term
t\in T_{\Sigma}
is irreducible for
R
if it is irreducible for
\mathop{\rightarrow}_{R}
.
If a term
t\in T_{\Sigma}
has a unique
\mathop{\rightarrow}_{R}
normal form, then the latter is
denoted by
t{\downarrow}_{R}
.
A GTRS
R
is reduced if for every rewrite rule
l\dot{\rightarrow}r
in
R
,

l
is irreducible for
R-\{\,l\dot{\rightarrow}r\,\}

and
r
is irreducible for
R
.
We recall the following important result.

Proposition 2.9

(
[
4
, Theorem 2.14]
)
.

Every
reduced GTRS
R
is convergent.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
E
and
F
be GTESs over a
signature
\Sigma
, and
W=ST\langle E\cup F\rangle
.
Then let

\Theta\langle E,F\rangle=\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cap W\times W
.

It should be clear that

\Theta\langle E,F\rangle
is an equivalence relation on
W
.
For each
t\in W
, we have
t/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}=t/_{\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}}\cap W\times W
.

Bottom-up tree automata
[
17
]
,
[
18
]

Let
\Sigma
be a signature, a bottom-up tree automaton (bta for short) over

\Sigma
is a quadruple
{\cal A}=(\Sigma,A,R,A_{f})
,
where
A
is a finite set of states of arity
0
,

\Sigma\cap A=\emptyset
,

A_{f}(\subseteq A)

is the set of final states,

R
is a finite set of rewrite rules of the following
form:

\sigma({b}_{1},\ldots,{b}_{k})\dot{\rightarrow}{b}
with
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}
,

b_{1},\ldots,b_{k},{b}\in A
.

We consider
R
as a GTRS over
\Sigma\cup A
.
The size of
\cal A
is denoted by
\mathrm{size}({\cal A})
, and is
|A|+\mathrm{size}(R)
.
The tree
language recognized by
\cal A
is
L({\cal A})=\{\,t\in T_{\Sigma}\mid(\exists{b}\in A_{f})\;t\mathop{\rightarrow}_{R}^{*}{b}\}
.
We say that
{\cal A}=(\Sigma,A,R,A_{f})
is a deterministic bta if
for any
\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}
,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
{b}_{1},\ldots,{b}_{k}\in A
, there is
at most one
rule with left-hand side
\sigma({b}_{1},\ldots,{b}_{k})
in
R
.

Proposition 2.11
.

[
7
]

Let
E
and
F
be GTESs over a signature
\Sigma
, and let

W=ST\langle E\cup F\rangle
.
For every
p\in W
,
we can construct in
O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
time the deterministic bottom-up tree automaton

{\cal A}=(\Sigma,C\langle E,F\rangle,R\langle E,F\rangle,\{\,p/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}\,\})
, and

{\cal A}
recognizes the tree language
p/_{\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}}
.

Proposition 2.12
.

[
10
]

Given two btas
{\cal A}=(\Sigma,A,R_{\cal A},A_{f})
and

{\cal B}=(\Sigma,B,R_{\cal B},B_{f})
,
we can decide whether
L({\cal A})\subseteq L({\cal B})
in
O(\mathrm{size}({\cal A})\cdot\mathrm{size}({\cal B}))
time.

Directed pseudographs without parallel arcs
[
19
]

A directed pseudograph is a type of directed graph that allows for both parallel arcs (i.e. pairs of directed arcs with the same tail and the same head) and loops
(i.e, directed arcs whose head and tail coincide)
[
19
, top of page 4]
.
A directed pseudograph without parallel arcs (dpwpa for short)
G
is an ordered pair
(V,A)
,
where
V
is a finite set, and
A\subseteq V\times V

is a relation on
V
. The set
V
is called the vertex set of
G
, and its elements
are called vertices. The set
A
is called the directed arc set of
G
, and its
elements are called directed arcs.
A walk in
G
is an alternating sequence
w=x_{1}\,(x_{1},x_{2})\,x_{2}\,(x_{2},x_{3})\,x_{3}\,\ldots\,x_{k-1}\,(x_{k-1},x_{k})\,x_{k}
,
k\geq 1
,
of vertices
x_{i}
and arcs
(x_{j},x_{j+1})
.
We say that
w
is from vertex
x_{1}
to vertex
x_{k}
. The length of
w
is the number
k-1
of its arcs
[
19
, page 12]
.
A single vertex
x_{1}
is considered as a walk from
x_{1}
to
x_{1}
of length
0
.
A trail is a walk
w
in which all arcs are distinct.
If the vertices of a trail
w
are distinct, then
w
is a path.
If the vertices

x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k-1}
of a trail
w
are distinct,
k\geq 2
, and
x_{1}=x_{k}
, then
w
is a cycle.
We say that a vertex
x
is reachable from a vertex
y
if there exists a walk
w
from vertex
y
to vertex
x
.
By definition, every vertex is reachable from itself. We say that a vertex
x
is positive-step reachable from a vertex
y

if there exists a walk
w
of positive length from vertex
y
to vertex
x
.
Observe that for any vertices
x
and
y
, the vertex
x
is positive-step reachable from the vertex
y
if and only if there exists a
path of positive length or a cycle from the vertex
x
to the vertex
y
.

The adjacency-list representation of a dpwpa
G=(V,A)
is an array
Adj
of
|V|
lists, one list for each vertex in
V

[
13
]
. For each
x\in V
,
the adjacency list
Adj(x)
contains all the vertices
y
such that there is a directed arc
(x,y)\in A
. That is,
Adj(x)

consists of all the vertices adjacent to
x
in
G
. In pseudocode we treat the list
Adj
as an attribute of the graph. Let us assume that we are given
the set

V
and the set of arcs.
It is well known that there exists an algorithm that builds the adjacency-list representation of the dpwpa
G
with a time complexity of
O(V)+O(A)
.

Congruence closure on subterm dags
[
4
]

Let
\Sigma
be a signature. A subterm dag over
\Sigma
is a directed, labeled, and acyclic multigraph
G=(V,A,\lambda)
, where
V
is the set of vertices,

A\subseteq V\times V
is the multiset of directed arcs and

\lambda:V\rightarrow\Sigma

is the labeling function such that if
outdegree(v)=m

for some vertex
v\in V
,
then
\lambda(v)\in\Sigma_{m}
. The multiset of directed arcs leaving a vertex is ordered. We denote by
v/i
the
i
th
successor of a vertex
v
.
The vertices of
G
can be interpreted as ground terms over
\Sigma
by the function
\widehat{\;}:V\rightarrow W
which is defined by recursion:
for a vertex
v\in V
, we have
\widehat{v}=\lambda(v)
if
outdegree(v)=0
and
we have
\widehat{v}=\sigma(\widehat{v/1},\ldots,\widehat{v/m})

if
outdegree(v)=m

for some
m>0
and
\lambda(v)=\sigma
. Let
E
be a GTES over
\Sigma
.
We say that
G

is a subterm dag for a GTES
E
if
\widehat{\;}
is a bijection
between
V
and
ST\langle E\rangle
.
Let
E
and
F
be GTESs over
\Sigma
.
We say that
G

is a subterm dag for the GTES
E
and GTES
F
if
\widehat{\;}
is a bijection
between
V
and
ST\langle E\rangle\cup ST\langle F\rangle
.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
E
and
F
be GTESs over a signature
\Sigma
,
G=(V,A,\lambda)
be a subterm dag for
E
and
F
. Then let

\tau\langle E,F\rangle=\{\,(u,v)\in V\times V\mid(\hat{u},\hat{v})\in E\,\}

and let
\rho\langle E,F\rangle
be the congruence closure of
\tau\langle E,F\rangle
.

We now cite
Proposition 3.4 in
[
4
]
.

Proposition 2.14

(
[
4
, Proposition 3.4]
)
.

Let
E
be a GTES over a signature
\Sigma
, and
n=\mathrm{size}(E)
.
We can construct
in
O(n)
time a subterm dag for the GTES
E
and
the relation

\tau\langle E\rangle
.

By Proposition
2.14
, we have the following result.

Statement 2.15
.

Let
E
and
F
be GTESs over a signature
\Sigma
, and
n=\mathrm{size}(E)+\mathrm{size}(F)
. We can construct
in
O(n)
time a subterm dag for the GTESs
E
and
F
.

Definition 2.16
.

Let
G=(V,A,\lambda)
be a subterm dag over
\Sigma
. An equivalence relation
\rho
on
V
is a congruence on
V
if for each ordered pair of vertices

u,v\in V
, the conditions
\lambda(u)=\lambda(v)
and
u/i\rho v/i
for each
1\leq i\leq outdegree(v)
imply that
u\rho v
.
Given a relation
\tau
on
V
, the smallest congruence on
V
containing

\tau
is called the congruence closure of
\tau
.

Let
\Sigma
be a signature, and
G=(V,A,\lambda)
be a subterm dag.
Then for any relation
\tau
on
V
, there exists a unique congruence closure of
\tau
on
V
,
and algorithms for calculating congruence closures
are given in
[
2
]
and
[
9
]
.
The latter can be implemented with a worst-case time of

O(|A|\,\mathrm{log}\,|A|)
;
the former runs in
O(|A|^{2})
time but seems to be faster in practice. We now apply the congruence closure algorithm of
[
9
]
.
Assume that it performs the weighted union operation
[
3
]
on equivalence classes. Then the congruence closure algorithm
keeps track the cardinality of each equivalence class during its run, and can output the cardinality of all
\rho
-classes. We rename the congruence classes
produced by the congruence closure algorithm from
1
to the number of the congruence classes in
O(|V|)
time.

Proposition 2.17
.

[
9
]

Let
E
be a GTES over
\Sigma
.
There exists an algorithm which, given a subterm dag
G=(V,A,\lambda)

for
E
, and a relation
\tau
on
V
, produces the congruence closure

\rho
of
\tau
on
V
in

O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
time,
where
n=\mathrm{size}(E)
. The algorithm gives each
\rho
-class a unique name in the set
\{\,1,\ldots,|V/\rho|\,\}
, and also outputs
an array of the cardinalities of all
\rho
-classes.

Let
E
be a GTES over
\Sigma
, and
G=(V,A,\lambda)

be a subterm dag for
E
, and
\rho
be a congruence on
V
.
We shall use a function
\mathrm{FIND}_{\rho}(x)
that returns a unique name of
the congruence class of
\rho
containing
x
for each
x\in V
.
A possible implementation of
such a function was described in
[
3
]
.

3
Running Examples for the Main Cases

Along the proof of our main result,
we shall distinguish four main cases.
We define these four main cases, and then we present eight running examples to illustrate our concepts and results. In each main case, a running example presents GTESs
E
and
F

such that
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}\subseteq\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
and a running example presents
E
and
F
such that
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}\not\subseteq\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
.
From now on throughout the paper,
E
and
F
are GTESs over a signature
\Sigma
, and
W
stands for

ST\langle E\cup F\rangle
.

Main Case 1:

\Sigma
is a unary signature. Example
3.18
and its sequels, Examples
5.51
,
7.72
,
10.105
. and
12.119
, and
12.124
, also Example
3.19
and its sequels,
5.52
,
7.73
,
10.106
,
12.120
, and
12.125

illustrate this case.

Main Case 2:

Both GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
and GTRS
R\langle F,E\rangle
are total.
Example
3.20
and its sequels
Examples
5.53
,
13.127
,
further
Example
3.21
and its sequels, Examples
5.54
,
13.128
,
exemplify this case.

Main Case 3:

\Sigma_{k}\neq\emptyset
for some
k\geq 2
, and at least one of
R\langle E,F\rangle

and
R\langle F,E\rangle
is total. Example
3.22
and its sequels, Examples
5.55
,
7.74
,
10.110
,
14.135
, and
14.142
, moreover
Example
3.23
, and its sequels, Examples
5.56
,
7.75
,
10.111
,

14.136
, and

14.143
present this case.

Main Case 4:

\Sigma
has a symbol of arity at least
2
,
and GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
and GTRS
R\langle F,E\rangle
are not total.
Example

3.24
and its sequel Example
5.57
, furthermore, Example

3.25
and its sequels Example
5.58
and Example
15.144

demonstrate this case.

Main Case 2 overlaps with Main Case 1 and Main Case 3, and is a subcase of the union of
Main Case 1 and Main Case 3.

For each running example,
we compute the sets
ST\langle E\rangle
,
ST\langle F\rangle
,
W
and the relations
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}
,
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
,
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}
,
\Theta\langle E,F\rangle
,
\Theta\langle F,E\rangle
,

\Theta\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
, and state whether

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}\subseteq\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
or
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}\not\subseteq\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
.

Recall that

E
and
F
are GTESs over a
signature
\Sigma
, and
W
stands for

ST\langle E\cup F\rangle
.

Example 3.18
.

Let

\Sigma=\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma_{1}
,
\Sigma_{0}=\{\,\#,\;\$\,\}
,

\Sigma_{1}=\{\,f,\;g\,\}
,

E=\{\,f(\#)\doteq g(\#)\,\}
, and

F=\{\,f(\$)\doteq g(\$)\,\}
.
Observe the following equations:

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}=\{\,(\delta(s),\delta(t))\mid\delta\in CON_{\Sigma,1},s,t\in\{\,f(\#),\;g(\#)\,\}\,\}
,

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}=\{\,(\delta(s),\delta(t))\mid\delta\in CON_{\Sigma,1},s,t\in\{\,f(\$),\;g(\$)\,\}\,\}
,

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}=\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
,

ST\langle E\rangle=\{\,\#,\;f(\#),\;g(\#)\,\}
,

ST\langle F\rangle=\{\,\$,\;f(\$),\;g(\$)\,\}
, and

W=\{\,\#,\;\$,\;f(\#),\;f(\$),\;g(\#),\;g(\$)\,\}
.

The equivalence classes of
\Theta\langle E,F\rangle
are

\{\,\#\,\}
,
\{\,\$\,\}
,
\{\,f(\#),\;g(\#)\,\}
,

\{\,f(\$)\,\}
,
\{\,g(\$)\,\}
.

The equivalence classes of
\Theta\langle F,E\rangle
are

\{\,\#\,\}
,
\{\,\$\,\}
,

\{\,f(\#)\,\}
,
\{\,g(\#)\,\}
,

\{\,f(\$),\;g(\$)\,\}
.

The equivalence classes of
\Theta\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
are

\{\,\#\,\}
,
\{\,\$\,\}
,

\{\,f(\#),\;g(\#)\,\}
,

\{\,f(\$),\;g(\$)\,\}
.

We note that
for each
t\in W
,

t/_{\Theta\langle{E\cup F},\emptyset\rangle}=t/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
or

t/_{\Theta\langle{E\cup F},\emptyset\rangle}=t/_{\Theta\langle{F},E\rangle}
.

That is, each
\Theta\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
equivalence class is equal to a

\Theta\langle E,F\rangle
equivalence class or a
\Theta\langle F,E\rangle
equivalence class.

Example 3.19
.

Let

\Sigma=\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma_{1}
,
\Sigma_{0}=\{\,\#,\;\$\,\}
,

\Sigma_{1}=\{\,f\,\}
,

E=\{\,f(f(\#))\doteq\#\,\}
, and

F=\{\,f(f(f(\#)))\doteq\#\,\}
.

For every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we define
f^{n}(\#)
as follows:

(i)

f^{0}(\#)=\#
and

(ii)

f^{n}(\#)=f(f^{n-1}(\#))
for
n\geq 1
.

Observe the following equations:

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}=\{\,(f^{m}(\#),f^{n}(\#))\mid m,n\in{\mathbb{N}},\;m-n\mbox{ is divisible by }2\,\}
,

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}=\{\,(f^{m}(\#),f^{n}(\#))\mid m,n\in{\mathbb{N}},\;m-n\mbox{ is divisible by }3\,\}
,

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}=T_{\Sigma}\times T_{\Sigma}
,

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}\subset\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}
,

ST\langle E\rangle=\{\,\#,\;f(\#),\;f^{2}(\#)\,\}
,

ST\langle F\rangle=\{\,\#,\;f(\#),\;f^{2}(\#),\;f^{3}(\#)\,\}
, and

W=ST\langle F\rangle
.

The equivalence classes of
\Theta\langle E,F\rangle
are

\{\,\#,\;f^{2}(\#)\}
,
\{\,f(\#),\;f^{3}(\#)\,\}
.

The equivalence classes of
\Theta\langle F,E\rangle
are

\{\,\#,\;f^{3}(\#)\}
,
\{\,f(\#)\,\}
,
\{\,f^{2}(\#)\,\}
.

The only equivalence class of
\Theta\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
is

\{\,\#,\;f(\#),\;f^{2}(\#),\;f^{3}(\#)\,\}
.

We note that

for each
t\in W
,

\left(t/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}\subset t/_{\Theta\langle{E\cup F},\emptyset\rangle}\mbox{ and }t/_{\Theta\langle{F},E\rangle}\subset t/_{\Theta\langle{E\cup F},\emptyset\rangle}\right)
.

Example 3.20
.

Let

\Sigma=\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma_{2}
,
\Sigma_{0}=\{\,\#,\,\$,\;\mathsterling\,\}
,

\Sigma_{2}=\{\,f\,\}
. Before
defining the GTESs
E
and
F
, we
present statements 1 – 4 as intuitive explanations.

1.
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}
has four congruence classes:

1.

The singleton
\{\,\#\,\}
. Because there is no equation in
E
with left-hand side or right-hand side
\#
. We consider
\#
as the representative
of this class.

2.

The set of all ground terms which are different from the constant
\#
, and contain only the symbols
f
and
\#
.
Applying the first three equations,
E
rewrites each ground term
t
of height
2
in the above set to
f(\#,\#)
.
Hence for each ground term
t
in this set, we have
t\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}f(\#,\#)
. We consider
f(\#,\#)
as the representative
of this class.

3.

The set of all ground terms containing only the symbols
f
and
\$
. Applying the fourth equation of
E

for each such ground term
t
in this set, we have
t\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\$
. We consider
\$
as the representative
of this class.

4.

The set of all ground terms which contain the constant
\mathsterling
or at least two different constants.
Let
s,t\in\{\,\#,\,f(\#,\#),\$,\mathsterling\,\}
. If the term

f(s,t)
contains the constant
\mathsterling
or at least two different constants, then
f(s,t)
is the left-hand side of one of the 5th – 15th equations of
E
.
Hence
for each ground term
p
which contains the constant
\mathsterling
or at least two different constants, we have
p\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\mathsterling
.

2. We obtain GTES
F
from GTES
E
by swapping the constants
\#
and
\$
. Intuitively we can think of
F
as a
“mirror image”
of
E
.

3.
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
has four congruence classes:

1.

the singleton
\{\,\$\,\}
,

2.

the set of all ground terms which are different from the constant
\$
, and contain only the symbols
f
and
\$
,

3.

the set of all ground terms containing only the symbols
f
and
\#
, and

4.

the set of all ground terms which contain the constant
\mathsterling
or at least two different constants.

4.
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}
has three congruence classes:

1.

the set of all ground terms which contain only the symbols
f
and
\$
,

2.

the set of all ground terms containing only the symbols
f
and
\#
, and

3.

the set of all ground terms which contain the constant
\mathsterling
or at least two different constants.

Let

E=\{\,f(\#,f(\#,\#))\doteq f(\#,\#),\;f(f(\#,\#),\#)\doteq f(\#,\#),\;f(f(\#,\#),f(\#,\#))\doteq f(\#,\#),\;\newline
f(\$,\$)\doteq\$,\;\newline
f(\#,\$)\doteq\mathsterling,\;f(\#,\mathsterling)\doteq\mathsterling,\;\newline
f(\$,\#)\doteq\mathsterling,\;f(\$,\mathsterling)\doteq\mathsterling,\;\newline
f(\mathsterling,\#)\doteq\mathsterling,\;f(\mathsterling,\$)\doteq\mathsterling,\;f(\mathsterling,\mathsterling)\doteq\mathsterling,\newline
f(\$,f(\#,\#))\doteq\mathsterling,\;f(\mathsterling,f(\#,\#))\doteq\mathsterling,\;\newline
f(f(\#,\#),\$)\doteq\mathsterling,\;f(f(\#,\#),\mathsterling)\doteq\mathsterling\,\}
,

F=\{\,f(\$,f(\$,\$))\doteq f(\$,\$),\;f(f(\$,\$),\$)\doteq f(\$,\$),\;f(f(\$,\$),f(\$,\$))\doteq f(\$,\$),\;\newline
f(\#,\#)\doteq\#,\;\newline
f(\$,\#)\doteq\mathsterling,\;f(\$,\mathsterling)\doteq\mathsterling,\;\newline
f(\#,\$)\doteq\mathsterling,\;f(\#,\mathsterling)\doteq\mathsterling,\;\newline
f(\mathsterling,\#)\doteq\mathsterling,\;f(\mathsterling,\$)\doteq\mathsterling,\;f(\mathsterling,\mathsterling)\doteq\mathsterling\newline
f(\#,f(\$,\$))\doteq\mathsterling,\;f(\mathsterling,f(\$,\$))\doteq\mathsterling,\;\newline
f(f(\$,\$),\#)\doteq\mathsterling,\;f(f(\$,\$),\mathsterling)\doteq\mathsterling\,\}
.

Note the following equations:

1.

ST\langle E\rangle=\{\,\#,\;\$,\;\mathsterling,\;\newline
f(\#,\#),\;f(\#,\$),\;f(\#,\mathsterling),\;\newline
f(\$,\#),\;f(\$,\$),\;f(\$,\mathsterling),\;\newline
f(\mathsterling,\#),\;f(\mathsterling,\$),\;f(\mathsterling,\mathsterling),\;\newline
f(\#,f(\#,\#)),\;f(f(\#,\#),\#),\;\newline
f(f(\#,\#),f(\#,\#)),\;f(\$,f(\#,\#)),\;f(\mathsterling,f(\#,\#)),\;\newline
f(f(\#,\#),\$),\;f(f(\#,\#),\mathsterling)\,\}
,

2.

ST\langle F\rangle=\{\,\$,\;\#,\;\mathsterling,\;\newline
f(\$,\$),\;f(\$,\#),\;f(\$,\mathsterling),\;\newline
f(\#,\$),\;f(\#,\#),\;f(\#,\mathsterling),\;\newline
f(\mathsterling,\$),\;f(\mathsterling,\#),\;f(\mathsterling,\mathsterling),\;\newline
f(\$,f(\$,\$)),\;f(f(\$,\$),\$),\;f(f(\$,\$),f(\$,\$)),\;\newline
f(\#,f(\$,\$)),\;f(\mathsterling,f(\$,\$)),\;\newline
f(f(\$,\$),\#),\;f(f(\$,\$),\mathsterling)\,\}
,

3.

W=\{\,\#,\;\$,\;\mathsterling,\;\newline
f(\#,\#),\;f(\#,\$),\;f(\#,\mathsterling),\;\newline
f(\$,\#),\;f(\$,\$),\;f(\$,\mathsterling),\;\newline
f(\mathsterling,\#),\;f(\mathsterling,\$),\;f(\mathsterling,\mathsterling),\;\newline
f(\#,f(\#,\#)),\;f(f(\#,\#),\#),\;\newline
f(f(\#,\#),f(\#,\#)),\;f(\$,f(\$,\$)),\;\newline
f(f(\$,\$),\$),\;f(f(\$,\$),f(\$,\$)),\;\newline
f(\$,f(\#,\#)),\;f(\mathsterling,f(\#,\#)),\;f(f(\#,\#),\$),\;f(f(\#,\#),\mathsterling),\;\newline
f(\#,f(\$,\$)),\;f(\mathsterling,f(\$,\$)),\;f(f(\$,\$),\#),\;f(f(\$,\$),\mathsterling)\,\}
.

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}=\{\,(\#,\#)\,\}\cup(T_{\{\,\#,f\,\}}\setminus\{\,\#\,\})\times(T_{\{\,\#,f\,\}}\setminus\{\,\#\,\})\cup(T_{\{\,\$,f\,\}}\times T_{\{\,\$,f\,\}})\cup\newline
\left(T_{\Sigma}\setminus(T_{\{\,\#,f\,\}}\cup T_{\{\,\$,f\,\}})\right)\times\left(T_{\Sigma}\setminus(T_{\{\,\#,f\,\}})\cup T_{\{\,\$,f\,\}})\right)
.

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}
has the congruence classes
\{\,\#\,\}
,
T_{\{\,\#,f\,\}}\setminus\{\,\#\,\}
,

T_{\{\,\$,f\,\}}
,   and

T_{\Sigma}\setminus(T_{\{\,\#,f\,\}}\cup T_{\{\,\$,f\,\}})
.

Then

1.

T_{\{\,\#,f\,\}}
is the set of all ground terms containing only the symbols
f
and
\#
.

2.

T_{\{\,\$,f\,\}}
is the set of all ground terms containing only the symbols
f
and
\$
.

3.

T_{\{\,\#,f\,\}}\cup T_{\{\,\$,f\,\}}
is the set of all ground terms which contain only the symbols
f
and
\#

or only the symbols
f
and
\$
.

4.

T_{\Sigma}\setminus(T_{\{\,\#,f\,\}}\cup T_{\{\,\$,f\,\}})
is the set of all ground terms which contain
the constant
\mathsterling
or at least two different constants.

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}=(T_{\{\,\#,f\,\}})\times T_{\{\,\#,f\,\}})\cup\left(T_{\{\,\$,f\,\}}\setminus\{\,\$\,\}\right)\times\left(T_{\{\,\$,f\,\}}\setminus\{\,\$\,\}\right)\cup\newline
\left(T_{\Sigma}\setminus(T_{\{\,\#,f\,\}}\cup T_{\{\,\$,f\,\}}\right)\times\left(T_{\Sigma}\setminus(T_{\{\,\#,f\,\}}\cup T_{\{\,\$,f\,\}}))\right)\cup\{\,(\$,\$)\,\}
.

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
has the congruence classes
T_{\{\,\#,f\,\}}
,
\{\,\$\,\}
,

T_{\{\,\$,f\,\}}\setminus\{\,\$\,\}
,  and

T_{\Sigma}\setminus(T_{\{\,\#,f\,\}}\cup T_{\{\,\$,f\,\}})
.

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}=(T_{\{\,\#,f\,\}})\times T_{\{\,\#,f\,\}})\cup(T_{\{\,\$,f\,\}}\times T_{\{\,\$,f\,\}})\cup\newline
\left(T_{\Sigma}\setminus(T_{\{\,\#,f\,\}}\cup T_{\{\,\$,f\,\}})\right)\times\left((T_{\Sigma}\setminus(T_{\{\,\#,f\,\}}\cup T_{\{\,\$,f\,\}})\right)
.

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}
has the congruence classes

T_{\{\,\#,f\,\}}
,
T_{\{\,\$,f\,\}}
,   and
T_{\Sigma}\setminus(T_{\{\,\#,f\,\}}\cup T_{\{\,\$,f\,\}})
.

Observe that
E\not\subseteq\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
and
F\not\subseteq\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}
and
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}=\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}
.

The equivalence classes of
\Theta\langle E,F\rangle
are

\{\,\#\,\},\;\newline
\{\,\$,\;f(\$,\$),\;f(f(\$,\$),\$),\;f(\$,f(\$,\$)),\;f(f(\$,\$),f(\$,\$))\,\},\newline
\{\,f(\#,\#),\;f(\#,f(\#,\#)),\;f(f(\#,\#),\#),\;f(f(\#,\#),f(\#,\#))\,\},\newline
\;\{\,\mathsterling,\;f(\#,\$),\;f(\#,\mathsterling),\;f(\$,\#),\;f(\$,\mathsterling),\;f(\mathsterling,\#),\;f(\mathsterling,\$),\;f(\mathsterling,\mathsterling),\;\newline
f(\$,f(\#,\#)),\;f(\mathsterling,f(\#,\#)),\;f(f(\#,\#),\$),\;f(f(\#,\#),\mathsterling),\;\newline
f(\#,f(\$,\$)),\;f(\mathsterling,f(\$,\$)),\;f(f(\$,\$),\#),\;f(f(\$,\$),\mathsterling)\,\}
.

The equivalence classes of
\Theta\langle F,E\rangle
are

\{\,\#,\;f(\#,\#),\;f(f(\#,\#),\#),\;f(\#,f(\#,\#)),\;f(f(\#,\#),f(\#,\#))\,\},\;\newline
\{\,\$\,\},\newline
\{\,f(\$,\$),\;f(\$,f(\$,\$)),\;f(f(\$,\$),\$),\;f(f(\$,\$),f(\$,\$))\},\;\newline
\{\,\mathsterling,\;f(\#,\$),\;f(\#,\mathsterling),\;f(\$,\#),\;f(\$,\mathsterling),\;f(\mathsterling,\#),\;f(\mathsterling,\$),\;f(\mathsterling,\mathsterling),\;\newline
f(\$,f(\#,\#)),\;f(\mathsterling,f(\#,\#)),\;f(f(\#,\#),\$),\;f(f(\#,\#),\mathsterling),\;\newline
f(\#,f(\$,\$)),\;f(\mathsterling,f(\$,\$)),\;f(f(\$,\$),\#),\;f(f(\$,\$),\mathsterling)\,\}
.

The only equivalence class of
\Theta\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
is
W
.

Example 3.21
.

We now define the GTESs
E
and
F
.
Intuitively, applying the first two rules, GTES
E
rewrites each occurrence of
\$
and
\mathsterling
to
\#
in every ground term
t\in T_{\Sigma}
. Then applying the last three rules, GTES
E
rewrites
the ground term
t\in T_{\Sigma}
with
height(t)\geq 1
to
\#
. GTES
F
rewrites each occurrence of

\flat
to
\mathsterling
in every ground term. In this way, it rewrites each ground term
t\in T_{\Sigma}
with
height(t)=1
to
\mathsterling
. Hence GTES
F
rewrites each ground term
t\in T_{\Sigma}
with
height(t)\geq 1
to
\mathsterling
.

Let

\Sigma=\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma_{2}
,
\Sigma_{0}=\{\,\#,\;\$,\;\mathsterling,\;\flat\,\}
,

\Sigma_{2}=\{\,f\,\}
,

E=\{\,\#\doteq\$,\;\#\doteq\mathsterling,\;\#\doteq f(\#,\#),\;\#\doteq f(\#,\flat),\;\#\doteq f(\flat,\#)\,\}
,

and

F=\{\,\mathsterling\doteq\flat,\newline
\mathsterling\doteq f(\#,\#),\;\mathsterling\doteq f(\#,\$),\;\mathsterling\doteq f(\#,\mathsterling),\;\newline
\mathsterling\doteq f(\$,\#),\;\mathsterling\doteq f(\$,\$),\;\mathsterling\doteq f(\$,\mathsterling),\;\newline
\mathsterling\doteq f(\mathsterling,\#),\;\mathsterling\doteq f(\mathsterling,\$),\;\mathsterling\doteq f(\mathsterling,\mathsterling)\,\}
.

We note that for each ground term
t\in T_{\Sigma}
with
height(t)\geq 1
,

t\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\#
and
t\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}\mathsterling
.

Therefore,

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}=\{\,(\flat,\flat)\,\}\cup((T_{\Sigma}\setminus\{\,\flat\,\})\times(T_{\Sigma}\setminus\{\,\flat\,\}))
,

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}=\{\,(\#,\#),\;(\$,\$)\,\}\cup((T_{\Sigma}\setminus\{\,\#,\;\$\,\})\times(T_{\Sigma}\setminus\{\,\#,\;\$\,\}))
, and

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}=T_{\Sigma}\times T_{\Sigma}
.

Note that

(\#,\flat)\not\in\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
. Consequently,

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}\subset\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}
.

We notice the following equations:

W=\{\,\#,\;\$,\;\mathsterling,\;\flat,\;\newline
f(\#,\#),\;f(\#,\$),\;f(\#,\mathsterling),\;f(\#,\flat),\;\newline
f(\$,\#),\;f(\$,\$),\;f(\$,\mathsterling),\;f(\$,\flat),\;\newline
f(\mathsterling,\#),\;f(\mathsterling,\$),\;f(\mathsterling,\mathsterling),\;f(\mathsterling,\flat),\;\newline
f(\flat,\#),\;f(\flat,\$),\;f(\flat,\mathsterling),\;f(\flat,\flat)\,\}
.

The equivalence classes of
\Theta\langle E,F\rangle
are

\{\,\#,\;\$,\;\mathsterling,\;\newline
\,f(\#,\#),\;f(\#,\$),\;f(\#,\mathsterling),\;f(\#,\flat),\;\newline
f(\$,\#),\;f(\$,\$),\;f(\$,\mathsterling),\;f(\$,\flat),\;\newline
f(\mathsterling,\#),\;f(\mathsterling,\$),\;f(\mathsterling,\flat),f(\mathsterling,\mathsterling),\;\newline
f(\flat,\#),\;f(\flat,\$),\;f(\flat,\mathsterling),\;f(\flat,\flat)\,\},\;\newline
\{\,\flat\,\}
.

The equivalence classes of
\Theta\langle F,E\rangle
are

\{\,\mathsterling,\;\flat,\;\newline
\,f(\#,\#),\;f(\#,\$),\;f(\#,\mathsterling),\;f(\#,\flat),\;\newline
f(\$,\#),\;f(\$,\$),\;f(\$,\mathsterling),\;f(\$,\flat),\;\newline
f(\mathsterling,\#),\;f(\mathsterling,\$),\;f(\mathsterling,\flat),f(\mathsterling,\mathsterling),\;\newline
f(\flat,\#),\;f(\flat,\$),\;f(\flat,\mathsterling),\;f(\flat,\flat)\,\},\newline
\{\,\#\,\},\{\,\$\,\}
.

The only equivalence class of
\Theta\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
is
W
.
We note that

\#/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}\subset\#/_{\Theta\langle{E\cup F},\emptyset\rangle}

and
\#/_{\Theta\langle{F},E\rangle}\subset\#/_{\Theta\langle{E\cup F},\emptyset\rangle}
.

Example 3.22
.

Let

\Sigma=\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma_{2}
,
\Sigma_{0}=\{\,\#,\;\$\,\}
,

\Sigma_{2}=\{\,f\,\}
,

E=\{\,f(\#,\#)\doteq\$,\;f(\#,\$)\doteq\$,\;f(\$,\#)\doteq\$,\;f(\$,\$)\doteq\$\,\}

and

F=\{\,f(\#,\#)\doteq\#\,\}
.

Note the following equations:

ST\langle E\rangle=\{\,\#,\;\$,\;f(\#,\#),\;f(\#,\$),\;f(\$,\#),\;f(\$,\$)\,\}
,

ST\langle F\rangle=\{\,\#,\;f(\#,\#)\,\}
,

W=ST\langle E\rangle
.

The equivalence classes of
\Theta\langle E,F\rangle
are

\{\,\#\,\}
,

\{\,\$,\;f(\#,\#),\;f(\#,\$),\;f(\$,\#),\;f(\$,\$)\,\}
.

The equivalence classes of
\Theta\langle F,E\rangle
are

\{\,\#,\;f(\#,\#)\,\},\;\{\,\$\,\},\;\{\,f(\#,\$)\,\},\;\{\,f(\$,\#)\,\},\;\{\,f(\$,\$)\,\}
.

The only equivalence class of
\Theta\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
is

\{\,\#,\;\$,\;f(\#,\#),\;f(\#,\$),\;f(\$,\#),\;f(\$,\$)\,\}
.

Observe that
E\not\subseteq\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
and
F\not\subseteq\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}
. Since
(\#,\$)\not\in\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
, and

(\#,\$)\in\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}
, we have
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}\subset\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}
.

Example 3.23
.

Let

\Sigma=\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2}
,
\Sigma_{0}=\{\,\#,\;\$\,\}
,
\Sigma_{1}=\{\,g\,\}
,

\Sigma_{2}=\{\,f\,\}
. We now define the GTESs
E
and
F
. Intuitively, applying the first five rules, GTES
E
rewrites each ground term
t
– where
height(t)\geq 1
and
t
contains only the symbols
f
,
g
, and
\#
– to
g(\#)
.
Aplying the last four rules, GTES
E
rewrites each ground term
t
containing an occurrence of
\$
to
\$
.
Let

E=\{\,f(\#,\#)\doteq g(\#),\;f(\#,g(\#))\doteq g(\#),\;f(g(\#),\#)\doteq g(\#),\;f(g(\#),g(\#))\doteq g(\#),\;g(g(\#))\doteq g(\#),\;\newline
f(\#,\$)\doteq\$,\;f(\$,\#)\doteq\$,\;f(\$,\$)\doteq\$,\;g(\$)\doteq\$\,\}
and

F=\{\,f(\#,\#)\doteq\#,\;g(\#)\doteq\#\,\}
.

Intuitively,
F
can simulate the first five equations of
E
, that is, the congruence generated by the first five equations of
E
is a proper subset of
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
.
We need the constant
\$
and the last four equations of
E
to ensure that

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\not\subseteq\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
.
Note the following equations:

1.

ST\langle E\rangle=\{\,\#,\;\$,\;f(\#,\#),\;f(\#,\$),\;f(\$,\#),\;f(\$,\$),\;g(\#),\;g(\$),\;f(\#,g(\#)),\;f(g(\#),\#),\;f(g(\#),g(\#)),\;g(g(\#))\,\}
,

2.

ST\langle F\rangle=\{\,\#,\;f(\#,\#),\;g(\#)\,\}
,

3.

W=ST\langle E\rangle
.

The equivalence classes of
\Theta\langle E,F\rangle
are

\{\,\#\,\},\{\,f(\#,\#),\;f(\#,g(\#)),\;f(g(\#),\#),\;f(g(\#),g(\#)),\;g(\#),\;g(g(\#))\,\},\;\newline
\{\,\$,\;f(\#,\$),\;f(\$,\#),\;f(\$,\$),\;g(\$),\;g(g(\$))\,\}
,

The equivalence classes of
\Theta\langle F,E\rangle
are

\{\,\#,\;f(\#,\#),\;f(\#,g(\#)),\;f(g(\#),\#),\;f(g(\#),g(\#)),\;g(\#),\;g(g(\#))\,\}
,

\{\,\$\,\},\;\{\,f(\#,\$)\,\},\;\{\,f(\$,\#)\,\},\;\{\,f(\$,\$)\,\},\;\{\,g(\$)\,\},\;\{\,g(g(\$))\,\}
.

The equivalence classes of
\Theta\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
are

\{\,\#,\;f(\#,\#),\;f(\#,g(\#)),\;f(g(\#),\#),\;f(g(\#),g(\#)),\;g(\#),\;g(g(\#))\,\},\;\newline
\{\,\$,\;f(\#,\$),\;f(\$,\#),\;f(\$,\$),\;g(\$),\;g(g(\$))\,\}
.

Observe that
E\not\subseteq\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
and
F\not\subseteq\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}
.

\$/_{\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}}=\$/_{\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}}=\{\,t\in T_{\Sigma}\mid\mbox{ there exists }\alpha\in POS(t)\mbox{ such that }t|_{\alpha}=\$\,\}
.

\#/_{\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}}=\#/_{\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}}=\{\,t\in T_{\Sigma}\mid\mbox{ there does not exist }\alpha\in POS(t)\mbox{ such that }t|_{\alpha}=\$\,\}
.

Accordingly
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}\subseteq\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
.

Example 3.24
.

Let

\Sigma=\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma_{2}
,
\Sigma_{0}=\{\,\#,\;\$\,\}
,

\Sigma_{2}=\{\,f\,\}
,

E=\{\,f(\#,\#)\doteq f(\#,\$),\;f(\#,\#)\doteq f(\$,\#),\;f(\#,\#)\doteq f(\$,\$)\,\}
and

F=\{\,\#\doteq\$\,\}
.

Observe the following equations and inclusion:

ST\langle E\rangle=\{\,\#,\;\$,\;f(\#,\#),\;f(\#,\$),\;f(\$,\#),\;f(\$,\$)\,\}
,

ST\langle F\rangle=\{\,\#,\$\,\}
,

W=ST\langle E\rangle
,

E\subseteq\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
,

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}=\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
,

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}\subseteq\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
.

Since
E\subseteq\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
, each equivalence class of
\Theta\langle E,F\rangle
is a subset of some equivalence class of
\Theta\langle F,E\rangle
.

The equivalence classes of
\Theta\langle E,F\rangle
are

\{\,\#\,\},\;\{\,\$\,\},\;\{\,f(\#,\#),\;f(\#,\$),\;f(\$,\#),\;f(\$,\$)\,\}
.

The equivalence classes of
\Theta\langle F,E\rangle
are

\{\,\#,\;\$\,\},\;\{\,f(\#,\#),\;f(\#,\$),\;f(\$,\#),\;f(\$,\$)\,\}
.

The equivalence classes of
\Theta\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
are

\{\,\#,\;\$\,\},\;\{\,f(\#,\#),\;f(\#,\$),\;f(\$,\#),\;f(\$,\$)\,\}
.

Example 3.25
.

Let

\Sigma=\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma_{2}
,
\Sigma_{0}=\{\,\#,\;\flat,\;\$,\;\mathsterling\,\}
,

\Sigma_{2}=\{\,f\,\}
,

E=\{\,\#\doteq\$\,\}
,
and

F=\{\,\mathsterling\doteq\flat\,\}
.

We remark that

f(\#,\mathsterling)\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}f(\$,\flat)
,

(f(\#,\mathsterling),f(\$,\flat))\not\in\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}
,   and

(f(\#,\mathsterling),f(\$,\flat))\not\in\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
.
Thence

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}\cup\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}\subset\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E\cup F}
.

Observe the following equations:

ST\langle E\rangle=\{\,\#,\;\$\,\}
,

ST\langle F\rangle=\{\,\mathsterling,\;\flat\,\}
,

W=\{\,\#,\;\$,\;\mathsterling,\;\flat\,\}
.

The equivalence classes of
\Theta\langle E,F\rangle
are

\{\,\#,\;\$\,\}
,

\{\,\mathsterling\,\}
,
\{\,\flat\,\}
.

The equivalence classes of
\Theta\langle F,E\rangle
are

\{\,\#\,\}
,
\{\,\$\,\}
,

\{\,\mathsterling,\;\flat\,\}
.

The equivalence classes of
\Theta\langle E\cup F,\emptyset\rangle
are

\{\,\#,\;\$\,\}
,

\{\,\mathsterling,\;\flat\,\}
.

Notice that
E\not\subseteq\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{F}
and
F\not\subseteq\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}
.

4
Fast Ground Completion Introducing Additional Constants

From now on throughout the paper, let
\Sigma
be a signature,
E
and
F
be GTESs over
\Sigma
,

W=ST\langle E\cup F\rangle
,
and
n=\mathrm{size}(E)+\mathrm{size}(F)
.
We recall the fast ground completion algorithm of Fülöp and Vágvölgyi
[
5
, Algorithm 3.6.]
. Its input is
\Sigma
,
E
, and

F
. Their fast ground completion algorithm
[
5
, Algorithm 3.6.]
produces in

O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
time a finite set of new constants and
a reduced GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle

over
\Sigma
extended with the new constants
such that
\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}=\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}\cap T_{\Sigma}\times T_{\Sigma}
.
Their algorithm calls a
congruence closure algorithm on the subterm dag for
E\cup F

[
9
]
, and runs in

O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
time by Proposition
2.17
.

We now cite the Claim on
[
1
, p. 167]
.

Proposition 4.26

(
[
1
, Claim on page 167]
)
.

\Theta\langle E,F\rangle=\{\,(\widehat{x},\widehat{y})\mid(x,y)\in\rho\langle E,F\rangle\,\}
.

Definition 4.27
.

We put

C\langle E,F\rangle=\{\,t/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}\mid t\in W\,\}
.

By Proposition
4.26
,
for each equivalence class
\vartheta\in W/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
, there exists exactly one equivalence class
\eta\in V/_{\rho\langle E,F\rangle}
such that

\vartheta=\{\,\widehat{x}\mid x\in\eta\,\}
.
To simplify our notations, we give
\vartheta

the unique name of
\eta
. The elements of
C\langle E,F\rangle

are viewed as constants, and we define the signature
\Sigma\cup C\langle E,F\rangle
.
By Definition
4.27
, we have the following statement.

Statement 4.28
.

|C\langle E,F\rangle|\leq|W|\leq\mathrm{size}(W)\leq n
.

Definition 4.29

(
[
5
, Section 3]
)
.

Let

R\langle E,F\rangle=\newline
\{\,\sigma(t_{1}/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle},\ldots,t_{m}/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle})\dot{\rightarrow}\sigma(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}\mid\sigma\in\Sigma_{m},m\in{\mathbb{N}},t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}\in T_{\Sigma},\sigma(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})\in W\,\}

be a GTRS over the signature
\Sigma\cup C\langle E,F\rangle
.

Note that for any
\sigma(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})\in W
with
\sigma\in\Sigma_{m}
,
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}\in T_{\Sigma}
there exists at most one
rewrite rule
with left-hand side
\sigma(t_{1}/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle},\ldots,t_{m}/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle})
in
R\langle E,F\rangle
.

Definition 4.30
.

We say that GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle

is total if for any
\sigma\in\Sigma_{m}
with
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
{b}_{1},\ldots,{b}_{m}\in C\langle E,F\rangle
, there exists a rewrite rule in
GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
with left-hand side

\sigma({b}_{1},\ldots,{b}_{m})
.

Fülöp and Vágvölgyi
[
5
]

showed the following properties of
R\langle E,F\rangle
.

Proposition 4.31

(
[
5
, Proposition 3.1]
)
.

R\langle E,F\rangle
is reduced.

By Proposition
4.31
and Proposition
2.9
, we have the following result.

Proposition 4.32
.

[
5
]

R\langle E,F\rangle
is convergent.

Proposition 4.33

(
[
5
, Proposition 3.2]
)
.

For every
t\in W
, we have
t\mathop{\rightarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}^{*}t/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
.

Proposition 4.34
.

[
5
]
Every
t\in W
has a unique
R\langle E,F\rangle
normal form.
For every
t\in W
, we have
t{\downarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}=t/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
.

Proof.

Let
t\in W
.
By Proposition
4.33
,
we have
t\mathop{\rightarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}^{*}t/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
.
By direct inspection of the rewrite rules of
R\langle E,F\rangle
, we get that for each

t\in W
,

t/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
is irreducible for
R\langle E,F\rangle
. Accordingly
t/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
is a
\mathop{\rightarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}
normal form
of
t
.
By Proposition
2.4
and Proposition
4.32
,

t\in W
has a unique normal form,

t{\downarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}
. Then we have
t{\downarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}=t/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
.
∎

Proposition 4.35

(
[
5
, Lemma 3.3]
)
.

For all
s\in T_{\Sigma}

and
t\in W
,

if
s\mathop{\rightarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}^{*}t/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
,
then
s\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}t
.

Proposition 4.36

(
[
5
, Theorem 3.4]
)
.

[
5
]

\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}=\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}\cap T_{\Sigma}\times T_{\Sigma}
.

Proposition 4.37

(
[
5
, Theorem 3.5]
)
.

For all
s,t\in T_{\Sigma}
,

s\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}t
if and only if
s{\downarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}=t{\downarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}
.

Proposition
4.34
and Proposition
4.37
imply the following proposition.

Proposition 4.38
.

For all
s\in T_{\Sigma}
and

t\in W
,

if
s\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}t
, then
s\mathop{\rightarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}^{*}t/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
.

By Proposition
4.35
and Proposition

4.38
, we have the following result.

Proposition 4.39
.

For all
s\in T_{\Sigma}
and
t\in W
,

s\mathop{\rightarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}^{*}t/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}

if and only if
s\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}t
.

Statement 4.40
.

If
R\langle E,F\rangle=\emptyset
, then
R\langle E,F\rangle
is not total.

Proof.

Let
\sigma\in\Sigma_{0}
. There does not exists a rule in
R\langle E,F\rangle
with left-hand side
\sigma
. Consequently,

R\langle E,F\rangle
is not total.
∎

Lemma 4.41
.

If
\bigcup W/_{\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}}=T_{\Sigma}
,
then

R\langle E,F\rangle
is
total.

Proof.

Let
\sigma\in\Sigma_{m}
with
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and let
t_{1}/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle},\ldots,t_{m}/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}

be equivalence classes
of
\Theta\langle E,\;F\rangle
for some
t_{1},\ldots t_{m}\in W
. By our assumption, there exists
s\in W
such that

\sigma(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}s
. By Proposition
4.37
,

\sigma(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}){\downarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}=s{\downarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}
. By Proposition

4.34
,

s{\downarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}=s/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
. Thus

\sigma(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}){\downarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}=s/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
.
Therefore,

\sigma(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})\mathop{\rightarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}^{*}\sigma(t_{1}{\downarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle},\ldots,t_{m}{\downarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle})=
(by Proposition
4.33
)

\sigma(t_{1}/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle},\ldots,t_{m}/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle})\mathop{\rightarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}s/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
(by direct inspection of the form of the rewrite rules of
R\langle E,F\rangle
).

By direct inspection of the rewrite rules of
R
, we get that
in the last step of the sequence of reductions we applied the rewrite rule

\sigma(t_{1}/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle},\ldots,t_{m}/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle})\dot{\rightarrow}s/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}

in
R\langle E,F\rangle
. ∎

Lemma 4.42
.

If

R\langle E,F\rangle
is a total GTRS, then for each
s\in T_{\Sigma}
,
there exists
t\in W
such that

s{\downarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}=t/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on

height(s)
.

Base Case:
Let

height(s)=0
.
Then
s=\sigma
for some
\sigma\in\Sigma_{0}
. By our assumption that
R\langle E,F\rangle
is a total GTES and by Definitions
4.29
and
4.30
,
there is a rewrite rule

\sigma\dot{\rightarrow}\sigma/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
in
R\langle E,F\rangle
.
Then
\sigma\in W
and
s{\downarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}=\sigma/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
.

Induction Step:

Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and let us assume that we have shown the
lemma for all integers less than or equal to
k
. Let
height(s)=k+1
.
Then
s=\sigma(s_{1},\ldots,s_{m})
for some
\sigma\in\Sigma_{m}
,
m\geq 1
, and
s_{1},\ldots,s_{m}\in T_{\Sigma}
.
By the induction hypothesis, for each
i\in\{\,1,\ldots,m\,\}
,
there exist
t_{i}\in W
such that

s_{i}{\downarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}=t_{i}/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
.
By our assumption that
R\langle E,F\rangle

is a total GTRS, there is a rewrite rule
with left-hand side
\sigma(t_{1}/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle},\ldots,t_{m}/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle})
in

R\langle E,F\rangle
.
By Definition
4.29
, this rule is of the form

\sigma(t_{1}/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle},\ldots,t_{m}/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle})\dot{\rightarrow}\sigma(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}

in
R\langle E,F\rangle
for some
p_{i}\in t_{i}/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
for every
i\in\{\,1,\ldots,m\,\}
.
Moreover
\sigma(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})\in W
.
Then

s=\sigma(s_{1},\ldots,s_{m})\mathop{\rightarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}^{*}s(t_{1}/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle},\ldots,t_{m}/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle})\mathop{\rightarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}\sigma(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
.

By direct inspection of Definition
4.29
, we get that
\sigma(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
is irreducible for
R\langle E,F\rangle
.
Thus

s{\downarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}=\sigma(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
. ∎

The following proposition is a
consequence of Lemma
4.41
and Lemma
4.42
and Proposition
4.37
.

Proposition 4.43
.

\bigcup W/_{\mathop{\leftrightarrow}^{*}_{E}}=T_{\Sigma}
if and only if

R\langle E,F\rangle
is
total.

Statement 4.44
.

GTRS

R\langle E,F\rangle
reaches a constant
{b}\in C\langle E,F\rangle
from a ground term
t
over the signature
\Sigma\cup C\langle E,F\rangle

if and only if
t{\downarrow}_{R\langle E,F\rangle}={b}
.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)

The constant
{b}\in C\langle E,F\rangle
is irreducibe for
R\langle E,F\rangle
by direct inspection of Definition
4.29
. By Proposition
4.32
,

{R\langle E,F\rangle}
is convergent.
Accordingly

{b}\in C\langle E,F\rangle
is the unique
R\langle E,F\rangle
-normal form of
t
.

(\Leftarrow)
This direction is trivial.
∎

Definition 4.45
.

Let

BSTEP\langle E,F\rangle=\{\,({b},{c})\in C\langle E,F\rangle\times C\langle E,F\rangle\mid\mbox{ there exists a rewrite rule }\sigma({b}_{1},\ldots,{b}_{m})\dot{\rightarrow}{b}\in R\langle E,F\rangle\mbox{ and }1\leq i\leq m\mbox{ such that }{c}={b}_{i}\,\}
.

We call an element of
BSTEP\langle E,F\rangle
a transition backstep.

Statement 4.46
.

Let
n=\mathrm{size}(E)+\mathrm{size}(F)
. Then

1.
|R\langle E,F\rangle|\leq|W|\leq\mathrm{size}(W)\leq size(E\cup F)\leq\mathrm{size}(E)+\mathrm{size}(F)=n
.

2.
\mathrm{size}(R\langle E,F\rangle)\leq\mathrm{size}(W)+|W|\leq 2n
and

3.
|BSTEP\langle E,F\rangle|\leq\mathrm{size}(R\langle E,F\rangle)\leq 2n
.

Proof.

By direct inspection of Definition
4.29
and by Statement
2.8
we have Statement 1.

We now show Statement 2. By Definition
4.29
,
for each rule
\sigma({b}_{1},\ldots,{b}_{m})\dot{\rightarrow}{b}\in R\langle E,F\rangle
, there exists a ground term
\sigma(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})\in W

such that
{b}_{i}=t_{i}/_{\Theta\langle E,F\rangle}
for every
i\in\{\,1,\ldots,m\,\}
.
Then we assign to the occurrence of
{b}_{i}
the root symbol of the ground term
t_{i}
for every
i\in\{\,1,\ldots,m\,\}
. Consequently
\mathrm{size}(R\langle E,F\rangle)\leq\mathrm{size}(W)+|W|
. By Statement 1,
\mathrm{size}(W)+|W|\leq 2n
.

By direct inspection of Definition
4.45
and by Statement 2 we have Statement 3.
∎

We now adopt the fast ground completion algorithm of
Fülöp and Vágvölgyi
[
5
]

that produces the GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
.

Definition 4.47

(
[
5
, Algorithm 3.6]
)
.

Algorithm Fast Ground Completion Introducing Additional Constants (FGC for short)

Input: A signature
\Sigma
, GTES
E
and GTES
F
over
\Sigma
.

Output:

1.

the relation
\rho\langle E,F\rangle
,

2.

the set of nullary symbols
C\langle E,F\rangle
,

3.

the GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
stored without duplicates in a red-black tree
RBT1
, and

4.

the relation

BSTEP\langle E,F\rangle
stored without duplicates in a red-black tree
RBT2
.

Data structures:
The congruence closure algorithm appearing in Proposition

2.17
, gives each
\rho
-class a unique name, which is an element of the set
\{\,1,\ldots,|W|\,\}
.
We assign to each symbol of
\Sigma
a unique number in the set

\{\,|W|+1,\ldots,2\cdot|W|\,\}
.

Let

\ell=\lceil\mathrm{log}_{2}|W|\rceil+1
.

For each symbol
\sigma\in\Sigma\langle E,F\rangle
, we
pad the binary form of its number
with leading zeros to length
\ell
. The binary sequence obtained in this way is called
the binary representation
of the symbol
\sigma\in\Sigma\langle E,F\rangle
, and is denoted as
\phi(\sigma)
.
Similarly, for each constant
{b}\in C\langle E,F\rangle
, we
pad the binary form of its number
with leading zeros to length
\ell
. The binary sequence obtained in this way is called
the binary representation
of the constant
{b}\in C\langle E,F\rangle
, and is denoted as
\phi({b})
.

The binary representation of a
rule
\sigma({b}_{1},\ldots,{b}_{m})\dot{\rightarrow}{b}\in R\langle E,F\rangle
is the string
\phi(\sigma)\phi({b}_{1}),\ldots,\phi({b}_{m})\phi({b})
.
We store the binary representations of the rewrite rules in
R\langle E,F\rangle
without duplicates in the red-black tree
RBT1
in lexicographic order.
The binary representation of an ordered pair
({b},{c})\in BSTEP\langle E,F\rangle
is the ordered pair
(\phi({b}),\phi({c}))
.
We store the binary representations of
the elements of
BSTEP\langle E,F\rangle
without duplicates in the red-black tree
RBT2
in lexicographic order.
Here
for every ordered pair

({b},{c})\in BSTEP\langle E,F\rangle
, the search key is the first component
\phi({b})
, the second component,
\phi({c})
is satellite data.

var

i
,
m
:
{\mathbb{N}}
;
\sigma
:
\Sigma
;
{b},{b}_{1},\ldots,{b}_{mar_{\Sigma}}:C\langle E,F\rangle
;
RBT1
,
RBT2
: red-black tree;

⬇

1. Create a subterm dag
G=(V,A,\lambda)
for
E
and
F
.

By Proposition
2.17
, compute the congruence closure
\rho\langle E,F\rangle
of the relation
\tau\langle E,F\rangle=\{\,(u,v)\mid(\hat{u},\hat{v})\in E\,\}.

Compute
C\langle E,F\rangle=\{\,x/_{\rho\langle E,F\rangle}\mid x\in V\,\}
.

2. { Computing the GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
}

R\langle E,F\rangle:=\emptyset
;

BSTEP\langle E,F\rangle:=\emptyset
;

For each
x\in V

do

begin

\sigma:=\lambda(x)
;

m:=outdegree(x);

{b}:=\mathrm{FIND}_{\rho\langle E,F\rangle}(x)
;

for each
1\leq i\leq m

do

{b}_{i}:=\mathrm{FIND}_{\rho\langle E,F\rangle}(x/i)
;

R\langle E,F\rangle:=R\langle E,F\rangle\cup\{\,\sigma({b}_{1},\ldots,{b}_{m})\dot{\rightarrow}{b}\,\}
;

BSTEP\langle E,F\rangle:=BSTEP\langle E,F\rangle\cup\{\,({b}_{1},{b}),\ldots,({b}_{m},{b})\,\}
;

end

By Statement
4.46
and Proposition
2.2
, the time complexity of
storing
R\langle E,F\rangle
without duplicates in the red-black tree
RBT1

is
O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
, and
the time complexity of storing

BSTEP\langle E,F\rangle
without duplicates in the red-black tree
RBT2
is
O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
, where
n=\mathrm{size}(E)+\mathrm{size}(F)
.

Proposition 4.48

(
[
5
, Algorithm 3.6]
)
.

Algorithm FGC takes as input a signature
\Sigma
, and GTESs
E
and
F
over
\Sigma
. Let
n=\mathrm{size}(E)+\mathrm{size}(F)
.

1. Algorithm FGC produces in
O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
time the relation
\rho\langle E,F\rangle
and
the set of nullary symbols
C\langle E,F\rangle
.

2. In
O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
time, Algorithm FGC computes
the GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
over
\Sigma\cup C\langle E,F\rangle
,
and stores
R\langle E,F\rangle
without duplicates in the lexicographic order
<_{lex}
on binary sequences
in the red-black tree
RBT1
.

3. In
O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
time, Algorithm FGC computes the relation

BSTEP\langle E,F\rangle
, and
stores
BSTEP\langle E,F\rangle
without duplicates
in the red-black tree
RBT2
. The search key is the first component, the second component is satellite data.

Proof.

Fülöp and Vágvölgyi
[
5
]
showed that we can construct the GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
in
O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
time.
Hence we can construct the relation
BSTEP\langle E,F\rangle
in
O(n\,\mathrm{log}\,n)
time as well.
In addition to the result of Fülöp and Vágvölgyi
[
5
]
, we now compute the cost of storing the GTRS
R\langle E,F\rangle
,
and the cost of storing the relation
BSTEP\langle E,F\rangle
.
First we show Statement 1.
Algorithm FGC produces the set of nullary symbols
C\langle E,F\rangle
. By Definition
4.27
,
for every equivalence class
\vartheta\in\Theta\langle E,F\rangle
,
Algorithm FGC gives a unique name for
\vartheta
which is the unique name of the
\rho\langle E,F\rangle
equivalence class
\eta
such that

\vartheta=\{\,\widehat{x}\mid x\in\eta\,\}
.

We now show Statement 2.
